It’s hard to explain because I know how it sounds, but that’s really what brings me in — that unreal feeling, like I’m observing myself rather than living it.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes where things feel unreal tend to happen when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I can say it only happens when I’m under pressure. I’ve noticed it most in places where I should feel completely normal, like my own house or my office, and that’s part of what makes it so disturbing. I’ll just be there, doing ordinary things, and then suddenly the room feels off — unreal, sort of distant — and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nI suppose stress might make it easier for it to happen, especially with work and everything, but the thing that stands out to me isn’t a clear stress trigger so much as that strange feeling of not quite being in my own life for a moment. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, but it’s not always like normal worrying about bills or work or something. It’s more like I’m constantly on alert, even when there’s no obvious reason to be.\n\nEven at home in Roanoke, where I should feel settled, I catch myself listening for every car door outside, every footstep on the porch, every little sound. I’ll notice where the exits are, even in places I know well, like I’m planning how I’d get out if I had to. It feels like part of me is waiting for someone to come at me again, and I know that sounds intense, but that’s honestly how it feels. I don’t really relax the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes where things feel unreal seem to be triggered by anything from your past?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off every time.” It doesn’t feel that neat.\n\nWhat I can say is that these moments are what really scared me enough to come in. I’ll be in my own house, or sitting in my office — places that should feel familiar and solid — and suddenly they don’t. The room feels strange, almost unreal, like it’s not quite my house or not quite my office anymore. And at the same time I feel like I’m not fully inside myself, like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo whether that’s tied to something from before… maybe, I don’t know. It feels like something in me is reacting, but I don’t always know to what. It’s just that when it happens, it’s very unsettling, because part of me knows where I am, but another part feels removed from it, like I’m not really present in my own life for those few moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant from your past?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’m avoiding specific places or people in that way. I’ve thought about it, because I know that might make sense, but I can’t really point to, you know, “I won’t go there because it reminds me of this,” or “I don’t talk to that person because of that.”\n\nI mean, there are times I feel uneasy in certain situations, but I don’t know that I’ve connected it clearly to something from the past. It’s more… I notice how I feel once I’m already somewhere, rather than planning my life around avoiding a particular place or activity. At least, not that I’ve recognized. Maybe I’m not seeing it clearly, but I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a traumatic event in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that in a clean way. I don’t have something I can just point to and say, “This is the traumatic event,” at least not something I feel ready or able to lay out clearly right now.\n\nI mean, I’ve had upsetting things happen in life, like anyone by my age, but I’m not sure what you would count as traumatic in the way you mean it. Part of me worries that if I say yes, then I’m making it sound bigger or more definite than I can explain. And if I say no, that doesn’t feel exactly right either, because obviously something has me shaken enough to be sitting here talking to you.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know. Or I’m not sure how to talk about it yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving a past event during one of these episodes where things felt unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the sense of suddenly being back in some specific moment and seeing it happen again, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhen those unreal feelings happen, it’s more like the present moment gets distorted somehow. Like I know I’m in my house, or I know I’m at work, but it feels off, unfamiliar, like I’m not quite connected to it. I don’t usually have a clear memory playing out, or a scene from the past that I’m reliving.\n\nI’ve tried to pay attention to that, actually, because part of me keeps wondering if there’s some pattern I’m missing. But so far it hasn’t been like, “Oh, I’m back there,” or “This is happening again.” It’s more vague than that. More like I feel removed from where I am, and then afterward I’m left trying to figure out what just happened and why it shook me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these unreal feelings happening more often after you've been in a situation that reminded you of something stressful or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say I’ve noticed a clear connection like that, but honestly, I haven’t been able to pin it down. That’s part of what’s bothering me. These moments happen in places that should feel completely normal to me, like my own house or my office. Places I know very well, places where I’ve spent years just going about my day.\n\nBut then suddenly it’s like the room isn’t quite real, or I’m not quite in it the way I’m supposed to be. Sometimes it feels almost like I’m watching myself from across the room, like I can see myself moving around or sitting there doing what I’m doing, but I don’t feel fully inside it. And that’s what brings me in, really, because it’s so unsettling when it happens somewhere familiar.\n\nI’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I reminded of something? Did something set it off?” But I can’t always find an answer. It isn’t like I walk into a situation and think, “This reminds me of something stressful,” and then the unreal feeling starts. It’s more that I’ll be at my desk, or at home doing something ordinary, and suddenly everything feels strange and distant. Then I end up trying to work backward and figure out why.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of what scares me about it. I wouldn’t say it feels like I literally float out of my body or anything dramatic like that, but there are moments where it feels like I’m not quite where I am. Like I’m in my own house, or sitting in my office, and suddenly the room feels unreal or distant somehow.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body. I know that sounds strange. I know I’m there, I know it’s my desk or my kitchen or whatever, but it doesn’t feel connected to me the way it normally does. It’s like I can see myself going through the motions, but I don’t feel fully inside the moment.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, because it keeps happening in places that should feel completely familiar. It’s unsettling, and then afterward I keep thinking, “What was that? Why did my own office suddenly feel like someplace I didn’t belong in?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where things feel unreal might be related to something traumatic that happened to you in the past?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. That’s the part I keep going back and forth on in my head.\n\nI can’t point to it and say, yes, this is definitely tied to something that happened before. What I can say is that what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office — suddenly feel unreal. Like I’m there, but not fully there. Almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside my own body, doing what I’m doing.\n\nAnd because it’s so unsettling, my mind naturally tries to explain it afterward. I’ll sit there thinking, “Was that because of something? Did something remind me of something else?” But in the moment, it doesn’t come with a clear memory or a clear connection. It’s more just this strange, disconnected feeling, and then afterward I’m left wondering whether there’s some link I’m not seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your daily life is mostly affected by these unreal feelings or by your general anxiety and worry?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the unreal feelings, at least in terms of what’s actually disrupting my life day to day. I mean, I do worry, of course I worry, but the thing that’s really getting in the way is that I don’t trust myself when those episodes happen.\n\nI prepare taxes for a living, so details matter. Deadlines matter. People are depending on me to catch things, to be accurate, to not miss something important. And lately I’ve missed several client deadlines, which is not like me. That’s not how I’ve worked for years. I keep sitting there with a return in front of me and then I start thinking, what if this feeling comes over me again and I’m not fully present? What if I make a mistake because I’m sort of watching myself instead of actually being grounded in what I’m doing?\n\nAnd I’ve stopped seeing people at the office right now, too. That’s hard to even say because it makes me sound unreliable, but I just don’t trust myself enough at the moment to handle clients face to face or to keep the workplace feeling safe and under control. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, probably — but I don’t feel steady enough. So yes, the worry is there, but the unreal feeling is what has really made me pull back and miss things I normally would never miss.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways, but that’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s not like I believe I’ve actually left my body. I know I’m physically there. I know I’m in my house, or I’m sitting in my office, or I’m standing by my desk. But in the middle of it, everything feels strange and far away, like the room isn’t quite real, or like I’m not quite connected to it.\n\nAnd then there’s this odd feeling of watching myself, almost from across the room. Like I can see myself doing normal things, but I don’t feel fully inside myself while it’s happening. It’s hard to explain without sounding ridiculous, but it’s like I’m present and not present at the same time. My own office can suddenly feel unfamiliar, even though I know perfectly well where I am.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. These moments where familiar places feel unreal, and I feel like I’m observing myself instead of just being there normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of feeling unreal or disconnected from your surroundings happen more frequently when you are under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure it’s only when I’m under a lot of stress. I mean, stress probably makes me notice it more, or makes me worry about it more afterward, but the episodes themselves… they can happen in places that should feel completely ordinary.\n\nThat’s what bothers me so much. I’ll be at home, in my own house, or at the office where I’ve spent years working, and suddenly it just doesn’t feel real in the normal way. It’s like I’m there physically, but something about the room feels distant or wrong, and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside the moment.\n\nSo yes, if I’m stressed, I’m probably more on edge about it happening. But what brings me in is really those moments themselves — my own house or office feeling unreal, and me feeling disconnected from myself while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a robot or automated when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like a robot, no… I don’t think that’s the word I’d use. It’s more that I feel removed from myself, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m mechanical,” or that I’m moving automatically like a machine. It’s more unsettling than that in a different way. I’ll be in my own house or sitting in my office, places I know very well, and suddenly they don’t feel quite real. And I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there inside the moment.\n\nSo I can still do things, or at least I think I can, but I don’t feel fully connected to doing them. It’s like there’s a distance between me and myself. That’s the part that keeps bothering me — not so much feeling robotic, but feeling like I’m observing myself in a place that should feel familiar and solid, and instead it feels unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected are the main problem you're experiencing, rather than anxiety or worry about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do worry about things, like anyone does, especially with work and just normal life, but that’s not really what made me feel like I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office, places I know inside and out — suddenly feel unreal. Not unfamiliar exactly, because I know where I am, but unreal, like there’s a layer between me and everything around me.\n\nAnd the strangest part is that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Like I can see myself sitting at my desk or standing in a room, but I don’t feel fully inside myself. That’s the thing that scares me and sticks with me afterward.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the main problem isn’t just everyday worrying. It’s these episodes where I feel disconnected from myself and from the place I’m in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes more often than you feel like you're just watching yourself from across the room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “outside my body” exactly. That sounds a little more extreme than how I usually experience it. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or looking down from the ceiling or anything like that.\n\nThe closest way I can describe it is more like… I’m in the room, but not fully in myself. Like I’ll be at my desk at the office, or standing in my own house, and suddenly the place feels unreal, almost like it’s not quite solid or not quite connected to me. And at the same time, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo if you’re asking which happens more, I’d say it’s more that “across the room” feeling. Not really outside my body in a literal sense, but definitely disconnected from being inside the moment. That’s the part that’s so hard to explain, because I know where I am, I know it’s my house or my office, but it doesn’t feel normal or real while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected from yourself and your surroundings are happening because you're trying to escape or cope with something else that's going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it feels like I’m trying to escape something, at least not in a conscious way. I’ve wondered about that, because it’s such a strange feeling that my mind wants to explain it somehow, like, “Am I avoiding something? Is this my brain shutting something out?” But when it happens, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t deal with this, so I’m going somewhere else,” if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I keep having these moments where my own house or my office — places that should feel completely ordinary to me — suddenly feel unreal. Like I know it’s my house, I know it’s my desk, I know where everything is, but it doesn’t feel like I’m quite there. And then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body, in my own life.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection to stress or something underneath it, I don’t know. I’m open to that. But from my side, it doesn’t feel like a deliberate coping thing. It feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to make sense of why everything familiar suddenly feels so distant and unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to deal with or get away from difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, honestly, because it would almost make more sense to me if I could say, “Yes, this happens when I’m trying not to feel something,” or “This happens when I’m overwhelmed by a thought.” But I can’t really point to it that clearly.\n\nWhat I can say is that what brings me in is these moments where my own house or my office — places I know completely, places I’m in all the time — suddenly don’t feel real in the way they should. I know where I am, I know nothing has actually changed, but it’s like everything is distant or off somehow.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not like I’m choosing to get away from something, more like I’m suddenly not fully connected to myself or the place I’m in.\n\nSo maybe it is my mind’s way of dealing with something difficult, I don’t know. But from inside it, it doesn’t feel intentional. It feels more like it just happens, and then I’m trying to catch up and understand why I feel so unreal in a place that should feel familiar.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But in the middle of it, everything feels strange and far away, like the room isn’t quite real, or like I’m not quite connected to it.\n\nAnd then there’s this odd feeling of watching myself, almost from across the room. Like I can see myself doing normal things, but I don’t feel fully inside myself while it’s happening. It’s hard to explain without sounding ridiculous, but it’s like I’m present and not present at the same time. My own office can suddenly feel unfamiliar, even though I know perfectly well where I am.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. These moments where familiar places feel unreal, and I feel like I’m observing myself instead of just being there normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of feeling unreal or disconnected from your surroundings happen more frequently when you are under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure it’s only when I’m under a lot of stress. I mean, stress probably makes me notice it more, or makes me worry about it more afterward, but the episodes themselves… they can happen in places that should feel completely ordinary.\n\nThat’s what bothers me so much. I’ll be at home, in my own house, or at the office where I’ve spent years working, and suddenly it just doesn’t feel real in the normal way. It’s like I’m there physically, but something about the room feels distant or wrong, and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside the moment.\n\nSo yes, if I’m stressed, I’m probably more on edge about it happening. But what brings me in is really those moments themselves — my own house or office feeling unreal, and me feeling disconnected from myself while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a robot or automated when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like a robot, no… I don’t think that’s the word I’d use. It’s more that I feel removed from myself, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m mechanical,” or that I’m moving automatically like a machine. It’s more unsettling than that in a different way. I’ll be in my own house or sitting in my office, places I know very well, and suddenly they don’t feel quite real. And I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there inside the moment.\n\nSo I can still do things, or at least I think I can, but I don’t feel fully connected to doing them. It’s like there’s a distance between me and myself. That’s the part that keeps bothering me — not so much feeling robotic, but feeling like I’m observing myself in a place that should feel familiar and solid, and instead it feels unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected are the main problem you're experiencing, rather than anxiety or worry about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do worry about things, like anyone does, especially with work and just normal life, but that’s not really what made me feel like I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office, places I know inside and out — suddenly feel unreal. Not unfamiliar exactly, because I know where I am, but unreal, like there’s a layer between me and everything around me.\n\nAnd the strangest part is that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Like I can see myself sitting at my desk or standing in a room, but I don’t feel fully inside myself. That’s the thing that scares me and sticks with me afterward.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the main problem isn’t just everyday worrying. It’s these episodes where I feel disconnected from myself and from the place I’m in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes more often than you feel like you're just watching yourself from across the room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “outside my body” exactly. That sounds a little more extreme than how I usually experience it. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or looking down from the ceiling or anything like that.\n\nThe closest way I can describe it is more like… I’m in the room, but not fully in myself. Like I’ll be at my desk at the office, or standing in my own house, and suddenly the place feels unreal, almost like it’s not quite solid or not quite connected to me. And at the same time, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo if you’re asking which happens more, I’d say it’s more that “across the room” feeling. Not really outside my body in a literal sense, but definitely disconnected from being inside the moment. That’s the part that’s so hard to explain, because I know where I am, I know it’s my house or my office, but it doesn’t feel normal or real while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected from yourself and your surroundings are happening because you're trying to escape or cope with something else that's going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it feels like I’m trying to escape something, at least not in a conscious way. I’ve wondered about that, because it’s such a strange feeling that my mind wants to explain it somehow, like, “Am I avoiding something? Is this my brain shutting something out?” But when it happens, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t deal with this, so I’m going somewhere else,” if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I keep having these moments where my own house or my office — places that should feel completely ordinary to me — suddenly feel unreal. Like I know it’s my house, I know it’s my desk, I know where everything is, but it doesn’t feel like I’m quite there. And then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body, in my own life.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection to stress or something underneath it, I don’t know. I’m open to that. But from my side, it doesn’t feel like a deliberate coping thing. It feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to make sense of why everything familiar suddenly feels so distant and unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to deal with or get away from difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, honestly, because it would almost make more sense to me if I could say, “Yes, this happens when I’m trying not to feel something,” or “This happens when I’m overwhelmed by a thought.” But I can’t really point to it that clearly.\n\nWhat I can say is that what brings me in is these moments where my own house or my office — places I know completely, places I’m in all the time — suddenly don’t feel real in the way they should. I know where I am, I know nothing has actually changed, but it’s like everything is distant or off somehow.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not like I’m choosing to get away from something, more like I’m suddenly not fully connected to myself or the place I’m in.\n\nSo maybe it is my mind’s way of dealing with something difficult, I don’t know. But from inside it, it doesn’t feel intentional. It feels more like it just happens, and then I’m trying to catch up and understand why I feel so unreal in a place that should feel familiar.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It could be, I guess. I mean, I can see why you’re asking it that way, because it does feel like some part of me is… not fully there, almost like my mind is stepping back from things. But I don’t experience it as, “I’m overwhelmed, so I’m going to shut this off,” or anything that clear.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep having these strange moments where things don’t feel real. Like, I’ll be in my own house — a place I obviously know — or sitting in my office, and suddenly it’s like I’m not fully there. It’s hard to explain without sounding odd, but it feels almost like I’m watching myself from across the room, like I’m observing everything instead of actually being inside my own life.\n\nIt’s unsettling. Like I’ll be in my house, or at my office — places I know perfectly well — and suddenly they feel strange or unreal, almost distant. It’s hard to explain because I know how it sounds, but that’s really what brings me in — that unreal feeling, like I’m observing myself rather than living it.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes where things feel unreal tend to happen when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I can say it only happens when I’m under pressure. I’ve noticed it most in places where I should feel completely normal, like my own house or my office, and that’s part of what makes it so disturbing. I’ll just be there, doing ordinary things, and then suddenly the room feels off — unreal, sort of distant — and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nI suppose stress might make it easier for it to happen, especially with work and everything, but the thing that stands out to me isn’t a clear stress trigger so much as that strange feeling of not quite being in my own life for a moment. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, but it’s not always like normal worrying about bills or work or something. It’s more like I’m constantly on alert, even when there’s no obvious reason to be.\n\nEven at home in Roanoke, where I should feel settled, I catch myself listening for every car door outside, every footstep on the porch, every little sound. I’ll notice where the exits are, even in places I know well, like I’m planning how I’d get out if I had to. It feels like part of me is waiting for someone to come at me again, and I know that sounds intense, but that’s honestly how it feels. I don’t really relax the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes where things feel unreal seem to be triggered by anything from your past?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I can’t point to one specific memory and say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off every time.” It doesn’t feel that neat.\n\nWhat I can say is that these moments are what really scared me enough to come in. I’ll be in my own house, or sitting in my office — places that should feel familiar and solid — and suddenly they don’t. The room feels strange, almost unreal, like it’s not quite my house or not quite my office anymore. And at the same time I feel like I’m not fully inside myself, like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo whether that’s tied to something from before… maybe, I don’t know. It feels like something in me is reacting, but I don’t always know to what. It’s just that when it happens, it’s very unsettling, because part of me knows where I am, but another part feels removed from it, like I’m not really present in my own life for those few moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant from your past?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that I’m avoiding specific places or people in that way. I’ve thought about it, because I know that might make sense, but I can’t really point to, you know, “I won’t go there because it reminds me of this,” or “I don’t talk to that person because of that.”\n\nI mean, there are times I feel uneasy in certain situations, but I don’t know that I’ve connected it clearly to something from the past. It’s more… I notice how I feel once I’m already somewhere, rather than planning my life around avoiding a particular place or activity. At least, not that I’ve recognized. Maybe I’m not seeing it clearly, but I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a traumatic event in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that in a clean way. I don’t have something I can just point to and say, “This is the traumatic event,” at least not something I feel ready or able to lay out clearly right now.\n\nI mean, I’ve had upsetting things happen in life, like anyone by my age, but I’m not sure what you would count as traumatic in the way you mean it. Part of me worries that if I say yes, then I’m making it sound bigger or more definite than I can explain. And if I say no, that doesn’t feel exactly right either, because obviously something has me shaken enough to be sitting here talking to you.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know. Or I’m not sure how to talk about it yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving a past event during one of these episodes where things felt unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the sense of suddenly being back in some specific moment and seeing it happen again, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhen those unreal feelings happen, it’s more like the present moment gets distorted somehow. Like I know I’m in my house, or I know I’m at work, but it feels off, unfamiliar, like I’m not quite connected to it. I don’t usually have a clear memory playing out, or a scene from the past that I’m reliving.\n\nI’ve tried to pay attention to that, actually, because part of me keeps wondering if there’s some pattern I’m missing. But so far it hasn’t been like, “Oh, I’m back there,” or “This is happening again.” It’s more vague than that. More like I feel removed from where I am, and then afterward I’m left trying to figure out what just happened and why it shook me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these unreal feelings happening more often after you've been in a situation that reminded you of something stressful or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say I’ve noticed a clear connection like that, but honestly, I haven’t been able to pin it down. That’s part of what’s bothering me. These moments happen in places that should feel completely normal to me, like my own house or my office. Places I know very well, places where I’ve spent years just going about my day.\n\nBut then suddenly it’s like the room isn’t quite real, or I’m not quite in it the way I’m supposed to be. Sometimes it feels almost like I’m watching myself from across the room, like I can see myself moving around or sitting there doing what I’m doing, but I don’t feel fully inside it. And that’s what brings me in, really, because it’s so unsettling when it happens somewhere familiar.\n\nI’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I reminded of something? Did something set it off?” But I can’t always find an answer. It isn’t like I walk into a situation and think, “This reminds me of something stressful,” and then the unreal feeling starts. It’s more that I’ll be at my desk, or at home doing something ordinary, and suddenly everything feels strange and distant. Then I end up trying to work backward and figure out why.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of what scares me about it. I wouldn’t say it feels like I literally float out of my body or anything dramatic like that, but there are moments where it feels like I’m not quite where I am. Like I’m in my own house, or sitting in my office, and suddenly the room feels unreal or distant somehow.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body. I know that sounds strange. I know I’m there, I know it’s my desk or my kitchen or whatever, but it doesn’t feel connected to me the way it normally does. It’s like I can see myself going through the motions, but I don’t feel fully inside the moment.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, because it keeps happening in places that should feel completely familiar. It’s unsettling, and then afterward I keep thinking, “What was that? Why did my own office suddenly feel like someplace I didn’t belong in?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where things feel unreal might be related to something traumatic that happened to you in the past?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. That’s the part I keep going back and forth on in my head.\n\nI can’t point to it and say, yes, this is definitely tied to something that happened before. What I can say is that what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office — suddenly feel unreal. Like I’m there, but not fully there. Almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside my own body, doing what I’m doing.\n\nAnd because it’s so unsettling, my mind naturally tries to explain it afterward. I’ll sit there thinking, “Was that because of something? Did something remind me of something else?” But in the moment, it doesn’t come with a clear memory or a clear connection. It’s more just this strange, disconnected feeling, and then afterward I’m left wondering whether there’s some link I’m not seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your daily life is mostly affected by these unreal feelings or by your general anxiety and worry?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s mostly the unreal feelings, at least in terms of what’s actually disrupting my life day to day. I mean, I do worry, of course I worry, but the thing that’s really getting in the way is that I don’t trust myself when those episodes happen.\n\nI prepare taxes for a living, so details matter. Deadlines matter. People are depending on me to catch things, to be accurate, to not miss something important. And lately I’ve missed several client deadlines, which is not like me. That’s not how I’ve worked for years. I keep sitting there with a return in front of me and then I start thinking, what if this feeling comes over me again and I’m not fully present? What if I make a mistake because I’m sort of watching myself instead of actually being grounded in what I’m doing?\n\nAnd I’ve stopped seeing people at the office right now, too. That’s hard to even say because it makes me sound unreliable, but I just don’t trust myself enough at the moment to handle clients face to face or to keep the workplace feeling safe and under control. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, probably — but I don’t feel steady enough. So yes, the worry is there, but the unreal feeling is what has really made me pull back and miss things I normally would never miss.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways, but that’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s not like I believe I’ve actually left my body. I know I’m physically there. I know I’m in my house, or I’m sitting in my office, or I’m standing by my desk. But in the middle of it, everything feels strange and far away, like the room isn’t quite real, or like I’m not quite connected to it.\n\nAnd then there’s this odd feeling of watching myself, almost from across the room. Like I can see myself doing normal things, but I don’t feel fully inside myself while it’s happening. It’s hard to explain without sounding ridiculous, but it’s like I’m present and not present at the same time. My own office can suddenly feel unfamiliar, even though I know perfectly well where I am.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. These moments where familiar places feel unreal, and I feel like I’m observing myself instead of just being there normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of feeling unreal or disconnected from your surroundings happen more frequently when you are under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure it’s only when I’m under a lot of stress. I mean, stress probably makes me notice it more, or makes me worry about it more afterward, but the episodes themselves… they can happen in places that should feel completely ordinary.\n\nThat’s what bothers me so much. I’ll be at home, in my own house, or at the office where I’ve spent years working, and suddenly it just doesn’t feel real in the normal way. It’s like I’m there physically, but something about the room feels distant or wrong, and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside the moment.\n\nSo yes, if I’m stressed, I’m probably more on edge about it happening. But what brings me in is really those moments themselves — my own house or office feeling unreal, and me feeling disconnected from myself while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a robot or automated when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like a robot, no… I don’t think that’s the word I’d use. It’s more that I feel removed from myself, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m mechanical,” or that I’m moving automatically like a machine. It’s more unsettling than that in a different way. I’ll be in my own house or sitting in my office, places I know very well, and suddenly they don’t feel quite real. And I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there inside the moment.\n\nSo I can still do things, or at least I think I can, but I don’t feel fully connected to doing them. It’s like there’s a distance between me and myself. That’s the part that keeps bothering me — not so much feeling robotic, but feeling like I’m observing myself in a place that should feel familiar and solid, and instead it feels unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected are the main problem you're experiencing, rather than anxiety or worry about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do worry about things, like anyone does, especially with work and just normal life, but that’s not really what made me feel like I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office, places I know inside and out — suddenly feel unreal. Not unfamiliar exactly, because I know where I am, but unreal, like there’s a layer between me and everything around me.\n\nAnd the strangest part is that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Like I can see myself sitting at my desk or standing in a room, but I don’t feel fully inside myself. That’s the thing that scares me and sticks with me afterward.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the main problem isn’t just everyday worrying. It’s these episodes where I feel disconnected from myself and from the place I’m in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes more often than you feel like you're just watching yourself from across the room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “outside my body” exactly. That sounds a little more extreme than how I usually experience it. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or looking down from the ceiling or anything like that.\n\nThe closest way I can describe it is more like… I’m in the room, but not fully in myself. Like I’ll be at my desk at the office, or standing in my own house, and suddenly the place feels unreal, almost like it’s not quite solid or not quite connected to me. And at the same time, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo if you’re asking which happens more, I’d say it’s more that “across the room” feeling. Not really outside my body in a literal sense, but definitely disconnected from being inside the moment. That’s the part that’s so hard to explain, because I know where I am, I know it’s my house or my office, but it doesn’t feel normal or real while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected from yourself and your surroundings are happening because you're trying to escape or cope with something else that's going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it feels like I’m trying to escape something, at least not in a conscious way. I’ve wondered about that, because it’s such a strange feeling that my mind wants to explain it somehow, like, “Am I avoiding something? Is this my brain shutting something out?” But when it happens, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t deal with this, so I’m going somewhere else,” if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I keep having these moments where my own house or my office — places that should feel completely ordinary to me — suddenly feel unreal. Like I know it’s my house, I know it’s my desk, I know where everything is, but it doesn’t feel like I’m quite there. And then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body, in my own life.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection to stress or something underneath it, I don’t know. I’m open to that. But from my side, it doesn’t feel like a deliberate coping thing. It feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to make sense of why everything familiar suddenly feels so distant and unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to deal with or get away from difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, honestly, because it would almost make more sense to me if I could say, “Yes, this happens when I’m trying not to feel something,” or “This happens when I’m overwhelmed by a thought.” But I can’t really point to it that clearly.\n\nWhat I can say is that what brings me in is these moments where my own house or my office — places I know completely, places I’m in all the time — suddenly don’t feel real in the way they should. I know where I am, I know nothing has actually changed, but it’s like everything is distant or off somehow.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not like I’m choosing to get away from something, more like I’m suddenly not fully connected to myself or the place I’m in.\n\nSo maybe it is my mind’s way of dealing with something difficult, I don’t know. But from inside it, it doesn’t feel intentional. It feels more like it just happens, and then I’m trying to catch up and understand why I feel so unreal in a place that should feel familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to unintentionally avoid feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: It could be, I guess. I mean, I can see why you’re asking it that way, because it does feel like some part of me is… not fully there, almost like my mind is stepping back from things. But I don’t experience it as, “I’m overwhelmed, so I’m going to shut this off,” or anything that clear.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where I’m in my own house, or I’m sitting at my office, somewhere I should feel completely normal and grounded, and suddenly it just feels unreal. Like the room is familiar but also somehow distant. I know where I am, I know it’s my desk or my living room, but it doesn’t feel like I’m properly in it.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not in a dramatic way, not like I’ve left my body, but like I’m observing myself going through the motions. So if that’s a way of unintentionally avoiding being overwhelmed, maybe. I’m not saying it couldn’t be. But from my side, it mostly feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to understand why a place I know so well suddenly feels so strange and not real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. These moments where familiar places feel unreal, and I feel like I’m observing myself instead of just being there normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of feeling unreal or disconnected from your surroundings happen more frequently when you are under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure it’s only when I’m under a lot of stress. I mean, stress probably makes me notice it more, or makes me worry about it more afterward, but the episodes themselves… they can happen in places that should feel completely ordinary.\n\nThat’s what bothers me so much. I’ll be at home, in my own house, or at the office where I’ve spent years working, and suddenly it just doesn’t feel real in the normal way. It’s like I’m there physically, but something about the room feels distant or wrong, and I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being inside the moment.\n\nSo yes, if I’m stressed, I’m probably more on edge about it happening. But what brings me in is really those moments themselves — my own house or office feeling unreal, and me feeling disconnected from myself while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a robot or automated when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like a robot, no… I don’t think that’s the word I’d use. It’s more that I feel removed from myself, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen it happens, I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m mechanical,” or that I’m moving automatically like a machine. It’s more unsettling than that in a different way. I’ll be in my own house or sitting in my office, places I know very well, and suddenly they don’t feel quite real. And I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there inside the moment.\n\nSo I can still do things, or at least I think I can, but I don’t feel fully connected to doing them. It’s like there’s a distance between me and myself. That’s the part that keeps bothering me — not so much feeling robotic, but feeling like I’m observing myself in a place that should feel familiar and solid, and instead it feels unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected are the main problem you're experiencing, rather than anxiety or worry about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do worry about things, like anyone does, especially with work and just normal life, but that’s not really what made me feel like I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office, places I know inside and out — suddenly feel unreal. Not unfamiliar exactly, because I know where I am, but unreal, like there’s a layer between me and everything around me.\n\nAnd the strangest part is that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Like I can see myself sitting at my desk or standing in a room, but I don’t feel fully inside myself. That’s the thing that scares me and sticks with me afterward.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say the main problem isn’t just everyday worrying. It’s these episodes where I feel disconnected from myself and from the place I’m in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes more often than you feel like you're just watching yourself from across the room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “outside my body” exactly. That sounds a little more extreme than how I usually experience it. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or looking down from the ceiling or anything like that.\n\nThe closest way I can describe it is more like… I’m in the room, but not fully in myself. Like I’ll be at my desk at the office, or standing in my own house, and suddenly the place feels unreal, almost like it’s not quite solid or not quite connected to me. And at the same time, I feel like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nSo if you’re asking which happens more, I’d say it’s more that “across the room” feeling. Not really outside my body in a literal sense, but definitely disconnected from being inside the moment. That’s the part that’s so hard to explain, because I know where I am, I know it’s my house or my office, but it doesn’t feel normal or real while it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected from yourself and your surroundings are happening because you're trying to escape or cope with something else that's going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it feels like I’m trying to escape something, at least not in a conscious way. I’ve wondered about that, because it’s such a strange feeling that my mind wants to explain it somehow, like, “Am I avoiding something? Is this my brain shutting something out?” But when it happens, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t deal with this, so I’m going somewhere else,” if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I keep having these moments where my own house or my office — places that should feel completely ordinary to me — suddenly feel unreal. Like I know it’s my house, I know it’s my desk, I know where everything is, but it doesn’t feel like I’m quite there. And then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body, in my own life.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection to stress or something underneath it, I don’t know. I’m open to that. But from my side, it doesn’t feel like a deliberate coping thing. It feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to make sense of why everything familiar suddenly feels so distant and unreal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to deal with or get away from difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, honestly, because it would almost make more sense to me if I could say, “Yes, this happens when I’m trying not to feel something,” or “This happens when I’m overwhelmed by a thought.” But I can’t really point to it that clearly.\n\nWhat I can say is that what brings me in is these moments where my own house or my office — places I know completely, places I’m in all the time — suddenly don’t feel real in the way they should. I know where I am, I know nothing has actually changed, but it’s like everything is distant or off somehow.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not like I’m choosing to get away from something, more like I’m suddenly not fully connected to myself or the place I’m in.\n\nSo maybe it is my mind’s way of dealing with something difficult, I don’t know. But from inside it, it doesn’t feel intentional. It feels more like it just happens, and then I’m trying to catch up and understand why I feel so unreal in a place that should feel familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes where you feel unreal or disconnected might be a way for you to unintentionally avoid feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: It could be, I guess. I mean, I can see why you’re asking it that way, because it does feel like some part of me is… not fully there, almost like my mind is stepping back from things. But I don’t experience it as, “I’m overwhelmed, so I’m going to shut this off,” or anything that clear.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep having these moments where I’m in my own house, or I’m sitting at my office, somewhere I should feel completely normal and grounded, and suddenly it just feels unreal. Like the room is familiar but also somehow distant. I know where I am, I know it’s my desk or my living room, but it doesn’t feel like I’m properly in it.\n\nAnd then I get that feeling like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. Not in a dramatic way, not like I’ve left my body, but like I’m observing myself going through the motions. So if that’s a way of unintentionally avoiding being overwhelmed, maybe. I’m not saying it couldn’t be. But from my side, it mostly feels like it just happens, and then I’m left trying to understand why a place I know so well suddenly feels so strange and not real."
  }
